He lets it sit every time it comes up, and keeps letting it sit long enough that people start bringing it up just to watch him do it again.


## Chapter Twenty-One: The Applications

*October 2022 – March 2023*

Chloe has been compared to the same ninety-one people since she was seven, and the only number the school has ever given her is where she comes in that ninety-one. She comes in the middle. Middle of her year gets what the middle of any year gets, which is the state school, so that is what she is expecting.

Everybody applies the same way. The Ivies first, eight of them, the way you buy a lottery ticket and then forget you own it. Then the college one of your parents went to. Then the community college an hour from home, which nobody says out loud but everybody lists. Then one strange one, picked for whatever reason a seventeen-year-old picks things: Bex applies to a place in Vermont because her cousin says the food is unbelievable, and Marek applies to one in Arizona because a woman he watched give a lecture online teaches there.


"MIT says four percent," Ruth says. She's got a laptop open on the arm of the couch in the third-floor common room, a board game abandoned since dinner still spread across the table between them.


"Four?" Sam has a card in one hand, his eyes on Ruth's screen instead.


"Four percent of applicants."


"That's not real," Sam says.


"It's on their site."


"It's marketing." He sets the card down. "A school wants to look exclusive, it puts a small number on the website. That's the whole trick. You sat through the same class I did."


"If a store put a sign in its window that said only four percent of the people who come in actually buy something," Chloe says, from the floor, a printed list of deadlines in her lap, "would you think the store was doing well, or badly run?"


"Neither." Ruth's eyes stay on the laptop. "You'd think the sign was doing a job that had nothing to do with the store."


"Or you'd want your money back for the trip in," Sam says.


Ruth is quiet for a second. "That's probably it," she says, and closes the laptop.


Chloe's list runs to fourteen. The eight, the state school, her mother's college, the community college, one in Michigan because Fen said the winters there are worth seeing once, and two she picks off a phone screen in about nine minutes after lights-out because they came up on the same page as the one in Michigan.

Her mother calls twice that month to ask how the essays are going and gets fine both times. The second time she leaves it there and reads the fourteen back down the phone, one at a time, and after each one she says the deadline out loud and waits for Chloe to say it back.


They do the essays in Ruth's room on a Sunday because doing them alone is worse. Sam takes the desk chair, Kavi the floor with his back against the bed, Odile the bed itself with her heels hooked over the frame, and Chloe sits under the window where the radiator is.

The first prompt wants six hundred words about a challenge she overcame. Chloe picks the year she taught the thirteens and had a boy in the front row who would not hand anything in, and she is eleven hundred words into it before she has got as far as the part where anything is overcome, because the interesting part is why he was right.

Ruth reads it over her shoulder before she can close the laptop. "That's not the assignment," she says, laughing at her. "They don't want an essay, they want a thing somebody can read in thirty seconds and still remember at lunch."

"I know what they want."

"Then why is it four pages."

"Because getting it to six hundred is harder than getting it to four thousand." Chloe cuts it three separate times before it holds at six hundred and eleven. Ruth, who spent a whole day on her first one and then did the other eleven in an afternoon, watches the last cut go and says that is the one she would have kept.

Sam finishes his in under an hour and is annoyed by how easy it was. "Ten pages a week for four years," he says, "and the thing that decides where I spend the next four is a paragraph. At that length they're guessing."

Kavi finishes in twenty minutes, exactly six hundred and one words, and submits it without reading it back.

Odile's runs four pages. Chloe tells her that is nearly seven times the limit and Odile says six hundred is a minimum, that every teacher in this building takes more and thanks you for it, and that hers is going in at four pages. It goes in at four pages.

Ruth's own prompt asks her to describe a time she failed, and she spends forty minutes on the word before she gets anywhere near an example. "Failed by whose standard," she says, half to the room.

"Pick one and write it down," Kavi says, eyes on his own screen.

"That's not an answer."

"It's a deadline."

A second prompt asks about a place that changed her. Chloe considers the forge and cannot get it down to six hundred words either, so she writes about the reservoir instead, nine miles out from her parents' house, and the hill near the top she has to stand up on the pedals to clear. It comes out at four hundred and ninety words and she does not have to cut a line of it, which she takes as a bad sign for about a minute and then submits anyway.

For the college her mother went to, the prompt asks why this school specifically. Chloe writes the honest, short answer instead of a longer invented one: her mother still has a sweatshirt from it, worn soft at the cuffs, at the back of a closet, and has only ever assumed Chloe would end up there too.


________________




The applications start landing in November.


The transcript is legal, an accreditation number printed in the corner. What's on it is classes, ordinary-looking rows of them, except there are so many of them that nine admissions offices decide it must be a formatting error and write back asking for the real one.


One of the nine calls comes through on a Thursday afternoon. "We're going to need the actual transcript," the caller says, polite and a little embarrassed, the way people get when they think they've caught somebody else's mistake. The registrar reads the accreditation number back to her, slowly, twice, and asks whether she'd like the same document mailed again. She declines and hangs up first.


Then somebody reads the essays.


What everybody notices first is that they read like graduate work, and what everybody thinks first is that somebody has written them. Ninety-one applicants out of one school, all at that level, is a mill or a very good teacher with a template. One review committee, reading blind, spends most of a meeting on the transfer-cohort theory before somebody checks the birth years.

Then they lay them side by side, which is what you do to catch a template, and the theory dies there. No two of them argue the same way. Two of the ninety-one take opposite positions on the same question and both are worth reading, which is the one thing a template can never produce.


An admissions officer at Penn works through a stack of files on a Tuesday morning, a mug going cold at her elbow. Odile's essay is three pages against a six-hundred-word limit, which is the sort of thing that normally gets a note in the margin and a mark against the applicant for not being able to follow an instruction. She writes the note. Then she keeps reading, because the three pages are plain and short on adjectives and do not read like somebody padding.

Two thirds of the way down the second page, as background to a point about patience, it mentions a self-defense assessment involving ten people.

The officer reads the sentence twice, sets the folder down, and reads it a third time before she picks up the phone. "Read this paragraph," she says to the office two doors down. "Tell me what you think it means." Her colleague reads it standing in the doorway, still holding his own coffee, and says he thinks it means exactly what it says. She says that is what she thought too. Two hours later, every file from the school is flagged.

By January there are open files on Halstead at more than thirty institutions.


________________




Eleven of the flagged schools send somebody.

The school says yes to all eleven. Desks in the long reading room, dining privileges at the same tables everyone already eats at, a room to work in with a door that locks, and permission to sit in any class they name, provided they name it in writing forty-eight hours ahead and a member of staff is in the room with them the whole time. There is a list of places closed to them: the research floors, and two rooms in the sports building that stay locked whether or not anyone is visiting.

They arrive within a week of each other that winter, from Yale, Stanford, Duke, Caltech, MIT and six others, and by the first evening they have found each other at one end of the visitors' table and started comparing notes the way scouts compare notes on the same prospect.

A representative from Duke asks what is in the sports building on his first afternoon, watching two students carry a crate through a door reserved for the people who work on the other side of it.

"Sport," the woman escorting him says.

He asks again two days later, over dinner, having established by then that she will not be embarrassed into more. "Athletics," she says, eyes on her plate.

He tries a third time on his last morning, one foot already in the car. "Recreation," she says, and shuts the door for him. He writes all three answers down in the order he got them, which is the only useful thing about them.

The Stanford representative asks for the cryptography elective because it is the class on the timetable he can least imagine existing, and sits through two hours of twelve-year-olds. He follows about a third of the material. What he tells a colleague afterwards is about the room rather than the material: that the questions came faster than the answers, that a girl at the back interrupted the teacher twice and was right the second time, and that nobody in the room appeared to think any of this was unusual.

The Yale representative asks for a senior writing seminar and gets Hearn, and watches him hand back an argument on the Federalist Papers with a single note in the margin. It reads, in full: *This hedges. Commit to the claim or drop it.* She asks afterwards how long the essay was. Ten pages, Hearn says, like most weeks.

The MIT representative asks for the metalworking elective and spends forty minutes watching thirteen-year-olds work stock at temperature. What he asks about, afterwards, is who taught them, and how long it took, and what they made in the first month. He is told, and writes none of it down until he is outside.

A rep corners Chloe in the library on a Wednesday and asks, pleasantly, what a typical day looks like. She tells him: five hours of class, two in the afternoon block, prep, dinner, watch on the nights she has it. He asks what watch is. She explains it the way she would explain a fire drill, because that is what she thinks it is, and he stops writing halfway through and asks her to say the part about the scoring again.

Another asks Sam what he is good at, expecting a list with some hedging in it.

"Fighting," Sam says. "I'm first in my year."

"How do you know?"

"There's a board." He says it the way you would say there is a clock. Then, because the number is his and he would rather it were accurate than impressive, he says the board only measures the drill, that the drill has a clock on it and a real one does not, and that Odile is second and would probably beat him in a room where nobody was counting.

The rep asks who Odile is. Sam points across the dining hall with his fork.

One evening a rep from Chicago tries a door on the research floor that is not supposed to open for him, and it does not. The door carries no sign, only a keypad and a camera above it. He mentions it at dinner and the Duke rep says he tried the same door on the Monday.

The Duke rep, still on his one-word answers, tries Ruth in the reading room one afternoon. "What's the hardest thing you've done here?"

"That's not a well-formed question," Ruth says, not looking up from her own laptop. "Hardest by what measure, and hardest compared to what?"

"Pick the measure you'd defend."

Ruth looks up at that.

________________

What breaks it, mostly, is the papers.

Three applicants have listed research on their applications, one line each, unelaborated. The school confirms all three exist, real titles, real dates, indexed where papers get indexed, and will not send a copy to anyone who asks. What it offers instead is a chair.

A representative may come and read the paper in a room, on paper, for as long as they like, with somebody in the room the whole time. No phone and no bag go in. The school provides the paper and a pencil and as much of its own notepaper as anybody wants, and everything written in that room is read before it leaves it. One committee calls the terms unworkable in an internal email, forwarded twice before somebody decides declining is simpler than arguing about it. Another leaves three separate follow-up requests to gather dust. Four accept.

The room is small and windowless, one lamp over a table. The Caltech representative sits down at nine in the morning and does not ask for anything else. The escort stays in the corner with a paperback of her own, checking the clock more than she checks him, and brings water at eleven.

By noon he has filled four sheets on both sides. At one she asks whether he wants lunch brought in and he says yes without looking up, and it goes cold at his elbow. By two he has stopped turning pages as often, reading the same few paragraphs over instead of moving forward. By three he is still on the page he was on at two, and at half past three he sets it down, looks at the wall for a while, and picks it back up.

He stands at ten to four, hands the sheets over to be read, and waits while she reads them.

"Is the third author available to talk?"

"She's sixteen," the escort says. "She's in class until four."

"Would four work?"

"No."

He shakes her hand, thanks her for the water, and asks if somebody can point him toward a taxi.

________________

Decisions arrive over nine days in March rather than all at once, which is the part everybody has braced for wrongly. at whatever hour each school decides, and the common room stops being a place anybody goes to relax. Ruth's first one lands at four in the afternoon on a Tuesday while she is in the middle of something else. Kavi is awake at midnight for one that turns out to be posting at seven the next morning, and he checks it at seven, in bed, before he gets up.

Ruth gets MIT and calls home, and her mother puts the phone on the kitchen counter on speaker so her father can hear it from the next room without stopping what he is doing. Kavi gets MIT, Caltech and Chicago, and is thinking about the next item on his list before dinner. Sam gets eleven of twelve and wants to know, immediately, what happened to the twelfth.

Odile gets in everywhere. She keeps it to herself, and it comes out three days later when Priya finds the letters stacked unopened in date order on her desk and says so at dinner in front of nine people.

Chloe gets twelve of fourteen.

Outside her window the snow along the path has gone soft and grey, the last of it, three weeks off the end of term. She sits on the floor of her room with the letters spread in two uneven rows, the ones that start with we are pleased and the two that don't. She reads the twelfth a second time before she believes the count, then a third, checking the name on the envelope against the name on the letter inside it in case an office somewhere has put two files together that both say Kessler.

Then she stops, because the arithmetic has gone wrong somewhere and it is not in the letters. The middle of a year gets the state school. She has twelve. Either the board that has been placing her since she was seven is measuring something these fourteen offices cannot see, or it has been measuring her against the wrong ninety people all along, and both of those are questions about the school rather than about her.

"It said four percent," she says, when Sam finds her there an hour later, still on the floor.

Sam comes in and sits down on the end of the bed with a sandwich he brought up from downstairs, and looks at the two rows for a while before he says anything.

"They lie. We established this."

"All of us, though?"

"How many did you get?"

"Twelve of fourteen."

"Right." He counts something off on his fingers, using the hand with the sandwich in it. "I got eleven of twelve, Ruth got everything she asked for, Kavi got three out of three. That's not four percent of anything." He points the sandwich at the letters. "So either every one of those places is lying on its website, or the number means something that isn't us."

"It can't be both."

"It can be both." He eats some of the sandwich. "It's marketing. Same thing the watch companies do."

Twice that week she takes the letters out again and counts them, and both times gets twelve. Then there's an exam on Thursday, and a language report due the Monday after that, and the letters go back in the drawer and stay there.


## Chapter Twenty-Two: The Offer

*April 2023*

Mr. Amberg runs the exit interviews himself, fifteen minutes each, ten or eleven students a day for nine days starting the first Monday of April. A list goes up outside the staff office every morning with that day's names in order, and a runner comes down from admin to call the next one in when the clock on his desk says it's time. He runs most of them the full fifteen minutes, by the staff's own count, even the ones that could have ended in under a minute. His own contracts class still meets four times that week, the interviews slotted into whatever's left of the day around it.


Chloe's slot falls on the Wednesday of the second week, third appointment after lunch. His office holds a desk, two chairs, and a small clock turned to face only him. A folder is already open on the desk when she sits down, her name typed across the tab, and he starts before she's settled.


"Four things," he says. "Research, full time, starting the day you'd otherwise be walking into a dorm room somewhere. Entry researchers elsewhere start around forty five thousand a year. You'd get three times that, minimum, more once you're carrying your own project instead of somebody else's. Housing, food, a car, medical, nothing coming out of your end for any of it. And if you want the degree on top of the work, you do it here, on your own schedule, we pay for it, and the post stays open until you finish, however long that takes. Six-day weeks the first year, five after that, same as anyone on staff. You'd report to whoever runs your team, same as anyone on staff. Nothing about being eighteen changes any of it."


"Here?"


"Here."


Forty five, three times over, is a hundred and thirty five. Housing on top of that. Food on top of that. A car, medical, all of it covered. Call the rest of it another twenty, a number he left out and she adds anyway. A hundred and fifty five thousand dollars a year, at eighteen, and she would still be here. She keeps both hands flat on her knees while she runs it, the way she keeps them flat on a table whenever she wants to get something right the first time.


The part she's actually weighing is different. She's taught the twelves real analysis out of a room two floors below this one, dropped three flawed hinges into a scrap bin and kept it to herself, and gone back to the forge every Thursday for five years before anyone had to make her. Money sits outside all of that. Eleven years ago the only thing that mattered about this place was that a letter had her name typed on the front of it. The folder open on the desk in front of her now has the same thing typed on the tab.


"I don't think so," she says.


"Reason?"


"It's my school." She takes a second to find the rest of it out loud. "Nobody stays at their school. You go and do the next thing."


Mr. Amberg writes something down and doesn't argue with her. He closes the folder, squares it against the edge of the desk, and checks the clock, which still has four minutes left on it. He spends them going back through the rest of the file in silence, the same slow read he'd give a mark scheme before handing it back, and closes it for good only once the four minutes are actually up.


At fifteen minutes exactly he stands. She stands too, and the next name is already waiting in the doorway.


________________




Ninety-one names go up over the nine days, and by the third one everyone in the building knows roughly what's behind the door before they sit down in front of it, because the ones who've already been through it talk in the corridor while they wait for the ones still ahead of it. By the sixth day the list needs a second sheet taped below the first, names running off the bottom of the original one in Amberg's own small, fast handwriting.


April keeps its ordinary shape around it. The twelves still have History at ten, the forge stays open Thursdays and both Saturday sessions, and lunch runs from noon to half past the same as every other week in the term.


Chairs line one wall outside his office, six of them, and there's always at least two people in them: whoever's next on the list taped to the door, and whoever's just come out, still deciding whether to go straight back to class or sit a while first. The corridor smells like the coffee cart that sets up near the stairwell every April, and the runner's shoes squeak the same two steps on the tile outside the office door, morning after morning.


Chloe's own wait that Wednesday runs eleven minutes, sandwiched between a boy re-reading the same page of a textbook, the words sliding past him each time, and a girl sitting perfectly still, hands in her lap, staring at the closed door like she's already rehearsed what's behind it. Another boy a few chairs down has a printed spreadsheet balanced on one knee, three different versions of the same arithmetic run down the margin, and folds it in half the second the door opens for him.


A girl from the materials wing comes out ahead of Chloe's own slot and sits down hard in the chair by the window, her bag still over one shoulder like she forgot to put it down, quiet for most of a minute. Then, half to herself, "I said I'd think about it." The boy next to her glances over once and goes back to his phone.


The boy in for the four o'clock slot is on his feet before the runner even calls his name, a laptop bag already over one shoulder like he's walking into a meeting rather than an office, and he's back out in six minutes flat, the door barely swinging shut behind him before it opens again.


"Six?" Chloe says, glancing at the door he just came out of.


"I already had the desk, since I was fifteen," he says, resettling the strap on his shoulder, still moving. "He just made it official." He's three steps past her by the last word, one hand up in a fast, half-formed wave.


By the second week the number stops being the interesting part of the conversation. Three times market, housing, a car, the degree paid for, the post held, everyone in the corridor runs some version of Chloe's own arithmetic out loud at some point and lands somewhere over a hundred thousand dollars, and after the fourth or fifth time it gets said out loud it barely gets a reaction from anyone still waiting in the chairs. Somebody chalks the whole sum onto the corner of the common room whiteboard by that Thursday, a hundred and thirty five plus everything else, and a second hand adds a question mark to the end of it before the day is out.


Two boys from the year below share the chair by the noticeboard for a few minutes between slots, close enough to overhear each other.


"You get the car thing?"


"Everyone gets the car thing."


The first one checks something on his phone before he bothers answering the follow-up. "I meant which car."


"He didn't say which car."


One boy, out of the office a full hour before Chloe's own turn, announces to the whole corridor that he turned it down flat because his mother would stop speaking to him for a decade if he skipped actual college for a paycheck. Three people laugh. He keeps reading whatever's on his phone, face flat, like the conversation already cost him something.


A girl two years below Chloe comes out grinning, drops into the chair by the door, and says the whole thing out loud twice, the number, the car, all of it, as if saying it again will make it hold still. The two boys waiting behind her let her have it, the volume included.


A boy from Chloe's own year, later in the second week, comes out and stays on his feet. He stands at the window with both hands flat on the sill until they stop shaking, then leaves before the question of whether he's all right gets an answer. The runner marks his name off the list a little more carefully than the others, and moves on to the next one.


What keeps being interesting, once the list is finished, is that eleven people said yes, and each of them had the answer before the walk back to class started. Every one of the eleven had already been somewhere in that building, on a floor with a badge on the door, longer than the rest of the year had any reason to know. The eighty who said no go back to their own last weeks of school, final transcripts still to sort out, the ordinary business of finishing something they'd already half finished before the list ever went up.


________________




Kavi is one of the eleven, and word gets around a plainer way than a posted list, before anyone official says a thing. Chloe hears his name in the common room before dinner, in the same breath as a number, and goes to get the rest of it from him directly instead of secondhand.


Chloe finds him in the network room that evening, back against the radiator by the door, a length of stripped cable coiled around one hand while he turns the copper end over between two fingers, over and over, eyes somewhere else. The overhead light in here has buzzed since October, low on anyone's list to fix.


"Why?" she asks.


"Because what I want to do is happening in one building and I'm standing in it," Kavi says, and keeps turning the cable.


"But it's school," she says.


"It stopped being school for me a while ago." He sets the coil down on the bench, edges lined up flush with the bench's own edge, and leaves it there. "School teaches you things on somebody else's schedule and grades you on how close you get to an answer that's already written down somewhere. Nobody upstairs has that answer written down. I brought them a false positive rate on Tuesday and nobody in the building could tell me if it was wrong, because nobody had checked it yet. That doesn't happen in a classroom. It can't. The classroom's whole job is already knowing." He looks at her the way he looks at a mark scheme, waiting for the next objection rather than the next compliment. "It's a lab that also has children in it." He says it flat, the same register he'd use to correct a wrong exponent, and moves on before it lands.


Chloe watches the cable a moment longer. It's the same thing the boy with the laptop bag already told her in six minutes, in fewer words. Kavi picks the cable back up and starts stripping the next foot of it, the same slow half turn of the wrist each time, and she leaves him to it.


________________




Chloe calls home that night, sitting on the floor of her room with her back against the bed, the phone warm against her ear before her mother even picks up.


Her mother picks up on the second ring, already three steps into her kitchen loop. "Hey, you. How'd it go today?"


"I said no."


"No to what?" A beat, the pacing not stopping yet. "Wait, what was the yes?"


Chloe gives it to her in order, the way Amberg gave it to her, four things instead of one long sentence. Research, full time. Three times the pay. Housing, food, a car, medical, none of it hers to pay for. The degree paid for if she wanted it, the post held after.


The pacing stops.


It starts again after a few seconds, three steps and back. "Three times what."


"He said forty five is what a researcher gets somewhere else. So a hundred and thirty five, before the rest of it."


"A hundred and thirty five," her mother says, "and a car."


"And medical. And the degree."


"How long's the contract."


"I don't know. I didn't ask."


"Would you have had to move out of the dorm."


"I didn't ask that either."


Her mother goes quiet again, the particular quiet she gets over a mortgage statement before she finds the one line that actually matters and reads it out loud twice. The line holds silent for the length of one more loop of the kitchen.


"Dave." Her mother's voice moves away from the phone and comes back closer, and then her father's on the line too, near enough that Chloe can hear both of them breathing at once.


"You said no," he says.


"Yeah." She picks at the frayed edge of the carpet while she waits for the follow-up she already knows is coming.


"What's the catch."


"There isn't one." She lets the quiet sit half a second before she gives him the rest of it. "I said it's my school. Nobody stays at their school, you go and do the next thing."


Both of them let it be. Her mother asks if she's sure three separate times across the next two minutes, in three different shapes, and gets the same answer each time, and eventually stops asking. Her father waits out the whole exchange before he asks whether anybody said yes.


"Kavi."


"Huh." A short laugh, cut off almost as soon as it starts. "Good for him."


"What about the other two." Ruth and Sam, he means, the only two names in that building already fixed in his memory.


"Same as me. Ruth's still going to MIT, and Sam's still doing the thing with the Army."


"Good," he says, the whole word behind it this time.


Before they hang up, her mother says the thing she'll go on saying every summer for the next two years, about the state school an hour from wherever Chloe ends up, about the cost being real, exactly as they've always said it was, and that they'll manage it the way they manage everything else. It comes out in the same flat voice she uses reading a mortgage clause out loud at the kitchen table, checking it against what it's actually going to cost.


"Okay," Chloe says.


"Okay," her mother says back, and there's the small click of her hanging up first.


Chloe sits there a while longer with the phone dark in her lap before she gets up to find something to eat.


## Chapter Twenty-Three: The First One

*June 2023*

Nobody at Halstead has ever done a graduation, and it shows.


Backstage, before any family has even found a seat, the ninety-one of them stand in a line that gets rearranged twice. Somebody assumes the order should go by height first. Then it gets fixed properly, alphabetical, once Ruth points out that alphabetical order is the one piece of the whole afternoon they actually rehearsed, and they've already gotten it wrong. The gowns come in three sizes for ninety-one different people, so half the line is cinched at the back with a binder clip, which somebody grabbed from the front office at the last minute. Sam is one of them, sleeves stopping three inches short of his wrists. The stairs up to the stage are steeper than anyone accounted for, no handrail screwed into place yet. The fourth or fifth student across catches a heel without quite falling, grabbing hard enough at whoever's handing out the certificates to leave a mark.


They put chairs out on the field, rows of them running well past where the field itself ends. Nobody who ordered them has ever had to guess how many parents, grandparents, and driving-distance cousins ninety-one students might produce between them. Off to one side stands a stage built that week, plywood over scaffolding, still smelling faintly of the primer somebody rolled onto it on Tuesday. Halfway through the ceremony an usher starts quietly folding up the emptiest rows and carrying them off. Whoever placed the order guessed for a crowd that never showed. This is the first graduation the building has run, and the guess was a blind one. The programs, printed that same week, are supposed to cover the whole crowd, but they run out before the P's, and the family behind Chloe's spends the second half of the alphabet sharing one with strangers.


The whole of the school below them sits on the grass behind the last row of seats, because there is nowhere else to put eight hundred children. The twelve-year-olds nearest the aisle spend the first twenty minutes kicking a water bottle down the gap between two empty rows, working out how many times it will go end over end before somebody's mother turns around.


The head of school gives a short speech that is mostly logistics: four years, some of them longer than four; ninety-one names; a line of thanks to parents, grandparents, and guardians, delivered in that order like an item list. There's one real sentence at the end, and it goes straight past Chloe, because Sam has spent the last two minutes trying to make her laugh and finally succeeds on the word "accomplishment." The microphone cuts out twice during it, and the second time whoever's holding it keeps talking for six more words before anyone notices, so half the field gets a graduation speech with a six-word hole in the middle of it. Whoever's running the sound board can be heard for three full seconds after the mic comes back, telling somebody named Carla that this whole thing was supposed to be tested Tuesday, before somebody realizes the levels are still up and cuts it again. The eight hundred kids on the grass, who rarely get a look at an adult from this school sounding rattled about anything, laugh hardest of all.


Ruth, three seats down from Chloe, counts the empty chairs still standing in the row ahead of them. "That's forty they'll need to clear before the applause even starts," she says. The chairs stay stacked exactly where they are. Sam laughs at the dead microphone before anyone's fixed it, loud enough that two rows turn around, and keeps laughing after everyone else has stopped. The mic still doesn't work.


Then they read out ninety-one names in alphabetical order, and the pacing is wrong from the start. Too fast through the A's, so the first few students are still finding their families in the crowd when the next name gets called. Then too slow through the back half of the alphabet, once whoever's reading catches the mistake and starts trying to correct for it. One name gets skipped clean between the D's and the E's. The girl has to be walked back up after the Z's for her turn, and gets more applause for it than anyone else all afternoon.


When Sam's name gets called, four rows of people who are not his family stand up anyway, Chloe's mother among them. Eleven years of pickups and drop-offs means she has watched this particular boy grow six inches in a single summer, and it still catches her off guard. When Ruth's name comes, half a row does the same thing for her. Priya's parents, an electrician and a woman who works the front desk at an urgent care clinic, clap for every single name, not just their daughter's, the whole way through.


Her mother cries from the K's onward, a tissue out three names early. Her father keeps one hand on the back of her chair the whole time.


________________


Her mother knows this campus. She's been coming for eleven years. She knows where the good parking is, knows Ruth's mother by name, and knows to bring a jacket because it's colder up here.


She finds Ruth's mother in the crowd before the ceremony even starts, and the two of them fall into the same easy back-and-forth they've had every year for over a decade. A supplier problem on one side, a form the school sent that neither of them has filled out yet on the other. "Is he in yet?" Ruth's mother asks, meaning the brother. "Any day now," Meg says. "He's already looking at dorms." Ruth's mother, who has forgotten her own jacket eleven years running, takes Meg's spare straight out of her hands. "You'll catch your death," Meg says, the same as every June before this one. Eleven years of parking in the same overflow lot, and neither of them has ever been inside the other's house.


A few rows over, Dave has already decided the stage is going to sag under the last few pairs of feet crossing it, and says so to the stranger sitting next to him. The stranger agrees with him, in real detail. It takes them both most of the conversation to work out that the stranger is Kavi's father. Somebody in a school polo comes by folding up an empty chair from the row behind them and apologizes for the state of things. "First time we've actually run one of these," she says. "It shows," Dave says, not unkindly, and goes back to the joists with Kavi's father.


Nadia watches an usher fold three more chairs, arms crossed, and lets him get on with it. It's somebody else's mess today, and somebody else's problem.


After, on the grass, with a paper cup of lemonade:


"You looked so tall up there."


"Mom."


"You did. Kavi's mother said the same thing." She fixes Chloe's collar. "I'm so proud of you I could be sick."


Her dad fills a memory card and gets none of her face, four hundred photographs of the backs of gowns and the tops of heads and, more than once, his own thumb. He lowers the camera long enough to ask her one real question. "So who's actually driving up in August, you or one of us?"


"Probably me," Chloe says. "I want the car for the fall anyway."


"Huh," he says, and lifts the camera again.


Her grandmother came, and sits in a folding chair at the end of a row, working through everyone who passes her like a receiving line that formed itself around the chair. Sam is the first one caught, gown still on over yesterday's t-shirt. She asks what he's doing next, and when he tells her the Army, she pats his arm and says it'll do him good, teach him some discipline. "Yes ma'am," Sam says, keeping the four-minute drill and the number he's been sitting on since he was fifteen to himself.


Kavi is next, in no hurry to get past her. She asks if he's staying on for more school. When he says he's staying at Halstead itself, she nods like that's the same thing and asks whether he'll get his own room out of it.


"Not yet," Kavi says.


"Well, that's something," she says, already looking past him for the next one.


The third one she catches is a stranger to Chloe, a girl from another family's cluster of chairs entirely, in a gown still cinched with somebody's binder clip. By the time the girl has extracted herself and gone to find her own people, Chloe's grandmother turns to Meg with the same flat satisfaction she'd bring to a verdict on a roast. "You all seem like very nice young people," she says, to the field at large, meaning all of them at once.


Not everyone gets caught. Before Chloe can get away too, her grandmother takes her hand, turns it over once in both of hers the way she always does, and takes in, silently, whatever new is there to find this time.


By the end of the afternoon the grass by the parking lot holds a small, accidental museum of everything a family brings to something like this: folding chairs people brought in preference to the ones provided, two coolers, a phone charger passed hand to hand down an entire row, a dog that shouldn't be there, and, near the back, a folding table somebody's grandfather set up to sell water bottles at cost, having decided he'd be the only one smart enough to bring any. He sells out in twenty minutes.


________________


Everything at the school keeps running: the labs are still going, the bell still sounds at ten past every hour. Eight hundred kids who are not graduating today are back in their seats before the folding chairs on the field have even finished coming down. By the time Chloe carries her own box past the language wing, the same twelve-year-olds who were kicking a water bottle an hour ago are already back at their desks. A substitute drills verb conjugations off a whiteboard, the same lesson that was scheduled weeks ago.


Chloe carries her own box down the ground-floor hallway, a forge mallet with a handle that's slightly the wrong shape wedged in next to a stack of notebooks. On her way out she reads the nine brass plates in the hall, the way she has every day for eleven years, and walks past them now the same way she always has.


________________


That evening, before anyone's driven off, or said goodbye, or signed anything, they are all still on the same patch of grass at the same time, in gowns still on from the ceremony. Someone has found a speaker from somewhere. Sam and Kavi are re-running an argument about volcanoes that's older than most of the certificates just handed out. Ruth is already on her third phone call about a dorm assignment. Theo takes a photograph of the group before anyone thinks to arrange themselves for one, more of them in it at once than end up in any other picture from that day.


Sam joins the Army. He's beaten every upperclassman he's sparred since ninth grade, and in July, at a recruiting office off the highway outside Pittsburgh, he signs the papers the way he laces his boots, quick and sure. The office smells like carpet cleaner and stale coffee, a flag in the corner leaning slightly off true. The recruiter slides four separate forms across the desk and starts explaining what each one means, and Sam is already signing the first one before the man finishes the sentence, but he reads the fourth form properly, twice, because it's the one about pay grade and he wants the actual number. He asks more questions about the physical assessment than about anything else on the form. "Anybody try to talk you out of this," the recruiter asks, more out of habit than curiosity. "No," Sam says, and means it exactly as flatly as it sounds. He walks out past the flag leaning off true and keeps his eyes ahead the whole way to the car.


Ruth goes to MIT, with a box of her own things labeled and half packed before the ceremony even starts, the label written in her own small, upright hand.


Nadia stays. She spends two months looking at jobs and comes out of it baffled. Then she goes back behind the counter of her parents' shop and checks the till before she says hello to anyone, same as she's always done.


She finds the discount in the ledger on a Tuesday, in her mother's own handwriting: ten dollars knocked off an hourly rate her mother has charged for six years. It matches a number a competitor three blocks over has been quoting all spring.


Nadia pushes her sleeves up before she brings it to her father.


"My mother knocked ten dollars off her own hours last week," she says.


"It is a cost," her father says, not looking up from the invoice in front of him.


"That's not a cost. That's the one thing in the shop that gets better every year." She's got the ledger open on the counter between them, one finger flat on the entry. "She's been quoting jobs since before I could reach this counter, and she still doesn't check what the guy three blocks up is charging before she writes a number down. Nobody's built the thing that would've caught it for her. I could build that."


Her father sets the invoice down and looks at her properly. "What would it even do?"


"Watch what the competitors charge. Flag it before she quotes, not after." She says it the way she'd say a fact about the weather.


He closes the invoice and takes a long moment before he answers.


She brings the same complaint to the chat that week, half a joke by the time it gets there. Eli says he'll look into it and doesn't, and by June she's started something of her own, and by September she's hiring.


Eli takes a job on a security team three states from where he grew up, and tells the group chat about it before he tells his mother. She finds out from a photograph he posts a month later, and is proud in the same uncomplicated way she's always been, contract or no contract.


Theo goes into the federal government as an analyst, having been interviewed four times and having answered every question completely. He keeps that photograph from the grass afterward, the one with more of them in it than any other, and keeps it to himself.


Kavi stays.


Ten others make the same choice, for their own reasons. Only Kavi says his out loud.


Priya takes a year and goes to South America with a bag. There's no work in it, no address on file yet, no return flight booked either.


"What's in the bag?" Chloe asks her, out on the grass, the two of them working through what's left of a cup of lemonade.


"Not much," Priya says.


"What are you doing when you get there?"


"Nothing, yet." She shrugs, working the strap of the bag between two fingers. "I've never had a whole year with nothing already decided for me. I want to find out what that's like."


Sam, half listening, tells her she's insane. Priya agrees, cheerfully, and keeps the bag exactly as light as it was.


Chloe leaves it there. She always has, with Priya, and this time is no different.


________________


By the time she's sitting in her own gown, Chloe already knows what the next two years look like, because she found out in May.


She reads the eligibility page for the Foreign Service exam a third time at her desk, to make sure the first two readings said what she thought they said. Twenty to sit it. Twenty-one to be sworn in, once the file behind it clears. She turns eighteen in August.


The math is fixed.


She closes the laptop that just told her the math, and leaves it shut for the rest of the night. Instead she goes down to the forge and works later than she has any real reason to. Past a certain point, the piece in front of her stops being about the piece in front of her.


She tells her father at the kitchen table once her mother's gone up, not because it's a secret from her mother but because this is the version of it that goes to him. Through the ceiling, water is running into the bath. Upstairs, it's still an ordinary Tuesday. The last of dinner sits between them on the table, foil pulled halfway over a plate of ham bones, which neither of them has bothered to clear yet.


She walks him through the whole of it, the way the page laid it out, rather than just telling him she can't sit the exam yet: the age floor, the training year sitting behind the floor once you clear it, and the number of separate stages between passing the written part and actually being sworn in. Then she shows him where, in that stack of stages, a person her age would already have to be standing for the numbers to come out anywhere close to twenty-one.


"How many stages," her father asks.


"Six, roughly. Written test, personal narrative, oral assessment, medical, security review, the roster itself. Nobody actually tells you how long any one of them takes until you're already inside it."


"So the two years in the middle," her father says, when she's finished, "that's a long two years to spend just waiting on one date. Find something in it you'd actually want to do anyway."


Chloe reaches for the last piece of ham on the table and eats it instead of answering.


Upstairs, the bath stops running. A few minutes later her mother comes down for a glass of water, sees them still at the table, and heads back upstairs, the question of what they were talking about left for another day.


So she takes the state school, an hour from where she'll be living, and the plan for the next two years is: wait.


## Chapter Twenty-Four: The Chat

*September 2023*

The chat is five years old. They wrote the encryption themselves at thirteen, mostly to keep a teacher from reading it while they arranged getting out of the building, and it's the only one any of them has ever used.


This is the first September that finds every one of them somewhere else. Eli, three states from where he grew up, has a new badge with last year's photo still on it, and Ruth is in a dorm room in Cambridge with a roommate she's met twice. Sam is at a processing station somewhere the paperwork won't name, where they hand his phone back a few minutes at a stretch. Kavi is still at Halstead, in a lab down the corridor from where he had his own lessons, doing work that used to be homework. Nadia is back behind the counter of her parents' shop, same as she's always done, while Theo, four states from the harbor town he grew up in, learns which parts of his new government job stay behind the badge. Priya is somewhere south of the equator, on a signal that comes and goes, and Chloe sits in a lecture hall built for four hundred people, an hour from the apartment she rents.


Eli types with his phone propped against a monitor, eyes staying on whatever's actually on the screen. Kavi answers from the same desk he does everything else from, one hand still turning over whatever object was already sitting on it. Ruth writes standing up, phone in one hand and a textbook open in the other, and finishes a whole exchange while keeping pace with whatever she was doing before it started. Nadia texts one-handed while she counts a till with the other, keeping track of both at once. Theo answers standing at a window with the blinds already down, phone held low and close, the way he'd hold a document above his clearance. Sam thumbs the keyboard with the same economy he uses on everything else, three words and stop, because someone behind him is already waiting for the phone. Priya replies from wherever she's charging it, propped against a boot, a bag, once against the neck of a horse that stood still for it.


Some of them answer inside a minute no matter the hour, Eli most nights, and Kavi from the same building he's always slept in. Others answer in bursts, three messages at once after a six-hour gap, a reply to something asked at nine landing after midnight because a shift only just ended. A message sent to the whole group at three in the morning gets an answer from somebody within five minutes. It has for nine years.


A message can sit read for hours, and it means only a shift running long, a supervisor still talking, a horse three switchbacks from cell service. Whatever he's in the middle of at the agency keeps running through a joke about somebody's boss, so Theo reads everything within minutes of it landing and sometimes waits a full day to answer it. Nadia reads fast and answers fast, except during the two hours after the shop closes, when she's doing the books and the phone goes face down on the counter. Sam goes dark for stretches at a time, the read receipt stuck wherever it last landed, and then three replies arrive inside the same minute once he's got the phone back. A message can open with guys, or ok, or silence before the next fact, arriving before whoever's reading has gotten past the first word.

## Chapter Twenty-Five: Ten Targets

*November 2023*

Sam likes the Army immediately.


The bus from the processing station gets in after dark, the first week of August, and the platform lights over the reception building draw every moth for a mile before anyone's off the bus. A sergeant with a clipboard reads names off a list in an order that follows some logic only the clipboard holds, and hands out bedding to whoever answers fast enough, and Sam is first off the bus and first to have his arms full of a folded blanket and a pillow with no case on it yet. He falls asleep that night to eighty other new privates finding out the same thing about their racks at the same time, and wakes before the lights do, already dressed and sitting on the end of the rack with his boots laced by the time the first bulb overhead flickers on.


The food is fine, the people are funny, and there is a schedule, which he has had every day since he was seven and briefly missed over the summer. He is polite to everybody. He is extremely polite to a sergeant named Ruiz, who shouts at him for four weeks and whom Sam likes enormously.


Ruiz has a voice built for the outdoors and no apparent interest in using an indoor one. On the second morning he goes down the line of racks with a boot in one hand, checking the fold on every blanket by eye, and stops at Sam's. "Marsh. This corner's a joke." He drags the blanket off with one motion and drops it on the floor. "Do it again." Sam does it again, hospital corners, the whole rack stripped and remade in under a minute, and Ruiz watches the second attempt with his arms crossed, then moves on down the line without another word, which from Ruiz is close to a commendation. Sam takes it as one. He tells the private in the next rack over, quietly, that he thinks Ruiz likes him. The private, still working on his own corners, says he's pretty sure that's not what just happened. Sam says he's pretty sure it is.


By the second week Ruiz has learned that yelling at Sam produces the same result as yelling at anyone else, which is his face staying exactly where it was and everything changing about what he does next, and he has apparently decided this is worth doing anyway, for its own sake, on principle, the way another man might keep polishing a boot that's already clean. "Marsh, you're smiling. Wipe it off." Sam wipes it off, and it comes straight back the second Ruiz moves down the line.


________________




He maxes the fitness assessment in week two. Then he does it again in week three, better, because in Sam's head a number he's already hit is just the number that comes next.


The morning of the first one they're on the gravel lot behind the barracks before the sun is properly up, breath showing, a folding table with a clipboard and a stopwatch set up at one end of it and a line of privates stamping their feet at the other. Sam counts himself down from three under his breath before the whistle goes, the same three-count he uses for everything that's going to cost him something, and then the deadlift bar comes up clean, and the sandbag goes where it's supposed to go, and the run finishes with him three lengths ahead of the next man and not especially out of breath about it. The soldier grading him reads the total off the sheet twice before writing it down, the way the captain will read a different sheet twice in four months' time, and asks him if he'd done this before. "No," Sam says. "I do other stuff." He keeps the rest of it to himself.


Word gets around the platoon by chow that afternoon, the way a number like that always does, and somebody two tables down asks him straight out whether he was some kind of college athlete. "No," Sam says. "Boarding school." The table waits for the rest of it. That's the whole of it, and the table goes back to eating.


The following week, on his own time and off the books, he does the whole test again with a buddy he's talked into holding the stopwatch, and beats his own number by enough that the buddy makes him check the watch twice before he'll believe it. That one stays off the sheet. Sam is satisfied regardless. He was doing it for the number, not the sheet.


He does that with everything. There's no version of a task anywhere in Sam's head where he reaches a number, decides that's enough, and stops there.


________________




September is ruck marches, longer every week, the packs weighed at the start of each one by a corporal with a fish scale who calls out the number like an auctioneer. Sam sets the straps once at the start and leaves them exactly there for the whole route, and finishes near the front of the line every time, breathing normally enough that the man behind him accuses him of cheating on the weigh-in. Sam holds his pack open for the corporal to check the scale a second time, just to settle it, watches the needle land in the same place it landed the first time, and goes back to walking, the pack riding exactly where he set it that morning.


Every Sunday, whatever the week has taken out of him, he sits on the end of his rack with a pad of paper and writes his grandmother a letter in block capitals pressed hard enough to dent the page underneath, folds it into a stamped envelope from the box she keeps sending him, and walks it to the mailbox himself rather than handing it to anyone. He tells her about the food and the cold and a sergeant who yells at everybody and means well by it. The fitness score stays out of the letter, since it wouldn't mean a thing to her, and the letters themselves stay his own business, a habit that goes the whole four months without anyone at the barracks catching him at it.


October is land navigation, a compass and a map board and six points to find in the dark before dawn, cold enough that the instructors hand out an extra layer before anyone asks for one, and Sam finds all six of his the first time out while two men in his squad are still arguing over a stream crossing on their board. He waits for them at the last point with his own already logged, sitting on his heels in the wet grass, and helps them find their fourth point on his own initiative, walking them through the bearing rather than just pointing at the map. By the third week the range weeks have gone from an event to a Tuesday. Sam qualifies early and spends the extra time on the line watching other people's groupings instead of his own, the same way he used to watch a climbing route he'd already cleared, looking for whatever's left in it. Ruiz catches him at it once, leaning on the barrier with his arms folded, and leaves him to it.


________________




In month four they put him on a range with ten targets and tell him to shoot ten targets.


The morning is flat and grey, the kind of cold that gets into the fingers before it gets into anything else, and the line of ten lanes sits under a low sky with the targets already up and waiting at distance. Sam counts himself down from three before the first one, same as always, and then works the line at a steady, unhurried pace, the bolt working, the brass falling into the same small drift of it at his feet with every round. He is aware, in the flat way he's aware of most things about himself, that this comes easily.


He shoots ten targets.


A captain he has not met before walks over afterward, looks at the sheet, and says, "That's the best score I've seen this cycle. What's your accuracy?"


"About forty," Sam says. The captain looks at the sheet again. It still says ten out of ten.


"Yeah. It's not great. Eighty's the standard, I'm working on it."


"Son, you hit ten out of ten."


"Right, but that's not accuracy," Sam says, and he says it kindly, because he has worked out that this must be a trick question and wants to let the man save face. "The target's not doing anything. It's just sitting there. That's not a measurement of anything, that's a measurement of whether the rifle works."


The captain doesn't say anything.


Sam takes that as an invitation, and this is the moment he has been waiting four months for, because he has become increasingly certain that basic training is some kind of extended screening exercise and that there is a real one somewhere and that he is being kept out of it by not having said the right thing yet.


"Accuracy is whether you can put a round into another round," Sam says. "In flight. On the count, opposite lanes, you both fire on the same beat. I'm at about forty percent and that's poor, there are people I went to school with at ninety. That's the number that means something." He nods at the rifle. "So I'm forty percent of a rifle."


There's a silence.


"Where did you go to school," the captain says.


"Halstead."


"Where's that?"


"It's a boarding school. It's small, you wouldn't have heard of it."


The captain looks at him for another moment, the sheet still in his hand, and then folds it once along a crease that's already there and puts it inside his jacket rather than handing it back. Sam takes that as the conversation ending, picks his brass up off the ground because leaving brass on the ground is one of the first things anyone here taught him not to do, and goes to find out what's next on the schedule.


________________




What gets written up that evening and forwarded twice by Friday turns Sam's own words into something else. The captain sits at a folding desk under a bare bulb with the range sheet smoothed flat in front of him and a cold cup of coffee pushed to one corner, and writes it out longhand first, crossing out the second paragraph twice before he's satisfied with it, sets the pen down, picks it back up, and adds one more line to the margin before he types the whole thing clean. The captain files it as a concern about the soldier rather than a recommendation for him.


It's four paragraphs. The first three describe what he did on the range. The fourth asks where an eighteen-year-old private learned to do it, contains the phrase appears to have received specialised instruction from an unidentified source, and recommends that the question be answered before he is put anywhere interesting. He reads it over once, signs it, and sends it up, out of his hands before he can second-guess it.


By the following Thursday it is being read in an office three floors up from anything with a range attached to it, by a woman three levels above the captain, at a desk clear of everything except the one folder open in front of her and a second stack squared off to one side. She reads the fourth paragraph twice, the way the grader read Sam's sheet twice, and sets the report on top of that second stack rather than into it, which is its own kind of answer, and reaches for the folder underneath it before the light outside her window has moved an inch.


None of it reaches Sam. Sam is having a great month. He gets a letter back from his grandmother the same week, three lines about the weather and a fourth asking whether the food's improved any, and he writes back that it's exactly as bad as it always was, and means it, perfectly content either way.


## Chapter Twenty-Six: The Exercise

*February 2024*

The exercise runs four days and Sam is on the receiving end of most of it.

Staging is a gravel lot behind a row of tents, a folding table under a work light, and a line of soldiers waiting their turn at it in the cold. Sam's breath fogs the whole time he's standing in it. Somebody behind him in the line is arguing about a missing glove liner. Somebody two places ahead of him gets handed a second flashlight when the first one won't come on.

When he gets to the front a sergeant hands him a compass, a red-lens flashlight, and a card with a six-digit grid on it. He'll be moving between two points on foot at night, the sergeant tells him. There's an opposing element working the ground between them, briefed to take him, and they pick their own timing, not him. Sam signs for the compass on a clipboard that's gone soft with damp, the pen skipping twice before it takes. He checks the bearing against the card, folds the card into his breast pocket, buttons the pocket, and heads for the tree line on his own initiative. The ground is frozen at the edges, enough to hold his boot print, and soft in the middle, enough to lose it. February does both at once, every year, on this range.

They come at him three times.

________________

The first is at a fence line a little after midnight on the first night: two strands of wire sagging between posts gone soft with rust, out past a stand of pines where the ground opens into a field left fallow a season or two back. Sam comes up on it slow, listening before he moves the last fifty feet. It's the way he's been taught since he was small enough that the drill still felt like a game. Two men are waiting on the far side of it in the dark, close enough to the posts that they read, for a moment, as more fence. They hold still.

Sam has the wire in his hand, easing it down to step over, when the nearer one comes off the post he's been leaning against. Sam gets a forearm up before the man's full weight lands, turns with it instead of against it, and puts him down into the wet grass on the near side of the fence. The second man is already moving. Sam meets him coming forward rather than waiting for him to arrive, catches the arm reaching for his collar, and uses the same turn a second time. Thirty seconds, maybe less.

He checks both of them, hand flat on each sternum, breathing fine, exactly the shape of the check he's been drilled to give. Then he's back over the fence and moving on the bearing, wire still humming faintly behind him where his boot caught it. He runs it back once, on the walk, decides it went fine, and leaves it at that.

________________

The second is the following night, in a culvert running under a service road, and this is the one that changes what he's doing with the rest of the exercise. The pipe is wide enough to walk through bent at the waist. Concrete's gone slick along the bottom where water's been standing in it for years, and it carries sound strangely, his own boots louder in his ears than they'd be in the open. He's committed to the culvert before he's read it properly, ducked in under the concrete lip because the ground on either side is open and he wants to be out of sight of it for the thirty feet it takes to cross. By the time he understands there are four men in there with him, the far end is already blocked, and so is the one behind him.

It takes about eleven seconds, which he only knows because someone times it and tells him afterward. He gets the first one moving before the man's weight is fully off the wall. He drives an elbow into the second, coming from his blind side. He takes a knee across the back of one leg to get to the third, low, ground where a fist swung at him would land on one of his own men instead. The fourth gets a thumb, deliberately or not he genuinely can't say afterward, bent back hard enough that Sam feels it go before he feels the pain of it, a white click somewhere behind his own eyes that arrives about half a second late. He's already moving when it happens; the thumb is a cost he pays on the way past, not a thing he stops for. He clears the far end of the culvert on his feet, checks that everyone behind him is still breathing the way he checked the two at the fence, and keeps going on the bearing, right hand curled loosely against his chest, because holding it open is worse.

The thumb swells to twice its size by the following evening and stays that way for a week. It's the eleven seconds that stay with him longer than the thumb does. He'd had the whole thirty feet of open ground to read before he committed to the culvert, and he went straight in anyway. That's the part he keeps going back to on the walk that night, turning it over the way he'd turn over a bad weld. Not what happened once he was inside; he can't fault that. The fifteen seconds before it, where he decided the culvert was worth the risk of not knowing what was in it.

________________

The third night is just walking, and the walking is its own kind of long. He crosses eleven miles of broken ground between the two points on the card. Most of it runs under a cloud layer thick enough that the red-lens light barely reaches his own boots, and the whole stretch of it passes quiet. Two contacts down, with a whole dark night still ahead of him, is a different kind of walking than the first two nights were. He feels the difference before he can put a name to it.

He starts taking the open ground the way the culvert taught him to. He stops at the edge of every tree line to listen before he crosses it, and goes wide around anything that reads like cover for two people instead of walking straight through it. Twice he stops dead for a full minute at a sound that turns out harmless once he's traced it back, a branch settling, an animal moving off through brush somewhere to his left. He stands there, weight balanced, breathing slow, until he's sure enough to move again. He eats standing, from a pouch, tearing the top off with his teeth and keeping his boots moving the whole time. By the last checkpoint of the night his hands are numb enough that reading the bearing off the compass takes twice the time it should. He reads it twice anyway before he trusts it.

________________

The fourth night is the one that goes in the report. Sam crosses a stretch of ground with the footing gone soft and uneven underfoot, an old drainage cut long since gone to rut and weed, at twenty minutes to four in the morning. He moves slower than he has all exercise, the ground holding him to it, one boot testing the next patch before he commits his weight to it.

What he has yet to learn, and what the debrief tells him the next afternoon, is that a man has been in position off his line of travel since a little after nine the previous evening. Six hours in one spot, through the whole drop in temperature that comes after midnight, waiting for Sam to come past a stretch of ground that the man worked out, correctly, was the only sane way through it. The man has the position, the timing, and the ground, with the dark behind Sam rather than in front of him. The entire encounter, start to finish, takes under two seconds.

Sam doesn't remember deciding anything about it. There's a shift of weight behind him, too sudden and too heavy to be the ground settling. Then there's a man's weight going where Sam wants it to go, off balance and down into the wet cut, arriving before Sam has finished registering that there's a man there at all. He checks him, hand on the sternum same as the other five, before his own pulse has caught up with what just happened. He stands there a moment longer than the check requires, looking down at the last thirty feet of ground behind him, ground he'd spent minutes reading his way across without once catching the man waiting on it. Then he keeps going on the bearing, because that's still the job.

The last checkpoint comes up grey rather than dark, the sky doing the slow work of turning into morning behind the tree line. Sam hands his compass and his card back at a folding table that's a twin of the one he signed for them at four days earlier, and a corporal checks the time on the card against a clipboard and waves him toward a tent with cots in it. He sleeps for five hours before anyone wants him for anything.

________________

The debrief runs the next afternoon in a low building with the windows painted over, a projector throwing a terrain overlay onto a screen at the front and two rows of folding chairs facing it, most of them full. Sam sits third row, ruck still on the floor between his boots, the swollen thumb resting on his knee, angled so he can watch it while seeming to watch the screen instead. A soldier two seats down keeps his eyes shut and his arms folded, saving whatever's left of the night before. Someone behind Sam works dried mud off a boot lace with a fingernail, steady, eyes down the whole time. The room smells of wet wool and the coffee urn somebody's set up on a table by the door, and the projector fan runs loud enough that the major raises his voice over it before he's finished the first sentence.

A major runs the room from a lectern at the front, working through the four days off notes on a clipboard, cross-referencing times against the overlay with a laser pointer that keeps drifting off the screen and onto the ceiling before he brings it back. He works through two other soldiers first, calling each one up by name to stand at the side of the screen while he reads their contacts back to them. One took a wrong bearing on the second night and lost four hours finding the checkpoint again. Another went the whole four days untouched, and stands there listening to his own good luck read out loud, looking more unsettled by it than a contact would have left him.

Sam's turn comes third, and he stays in his seat for it, the major working straight off the overlay and addressing him from the lectern instead. The fence line takes about a minute, the culvert less, times read off both like a man confirming a delivery arrived. He stops longer on the third contact, on the six hours the man spent in position and the two seconds it took once Sam actually arrived, and reads both numbers out to the room like they're supposed to mean something on their own.

Sam waits through it, because he assumes there's a critique coming, and he's got one ready for himself about the culvert, the fifteen seconds before the eleven he already knows about.

"Any questions," the major says, at the end, closing the clipboard.

"Yeah." Sam sits forward, ruck creaking under his boot. "Was that the assessment, or was that still setup?"

The major's eyebrows go up. Somebody two rows back stops picking at the boot lace.

"Because if that's the assessment I've got notes," Sam says. "The culvert one was bad. I let them have the back door and that's on me, I should have gone up and around and I got lazy about the time. If we're doing it again I'd want it harder, honestly, or at least more of them, because a two, a four, and a one over four days is a lot of walking for not much."

The major looks at him across the width of the room for a while, laser pointer off now, clipboard shut under his arm. Then he sets the clipboard down on the lectern, checks his watch, and calls the next name on his list.

It goes upward anyway.

## Chapter Twenty-Seven: Nadia

*May 2024*

Somebody tries to run an operation against Nadia's company in the spring.


She's forty people and eleven months old, known only within three counties, which is exactly as far as word travels when the product is a reader that plugs into a fifteen-year-old sedan and tells an independent mechanic what's actually wrong with it instead of what the manufacturer's own software wants him to pay a dealership to find out. The building used to be a fastener warehouse: one long room with a half-wall down the middle, engineers on one side of it and assembly on the other, a loading dock that's sat idle since the second week with the roll door pinned open on a cinder block, and a stretch of concrete floor she resurfaced herself over a weekend because the original slab was cracking under the bench legs. Forty people means forty coffee orders a morning and one machine in the break room that's tasted like the inside of its own box since the day it arrived, because fixing it keeps losing to everything else on the list. It also means a cleaning contract, four nights a week, the same crew that does a dentist's office and a chiropractor's on the same lot. Nadia interviewed them herself when she signed it, the way she interviews everyone who gets a key to the building, and gave it no more thought until the second week of May. She still runs the floor the way she ran the third-floor drill: five minutes standing on a chair at eight each morning, telling forty people what shipped yesterday and what's due today, and then she's down among the benches for the rest of it, closer to the problem than anyone else in the room.

She's still on the floor at nine that night because a batch of housings came back from the machine shop half a millimeter off tolerance and she wants to see the problem herself before she calls anyone about it. The cleaner works the far end of the room first, same as always, and she's most of the way through the tolerance report when she notices he's stopped moving. He's standing still at the test bench, rag slack in one hand, a board turned over in the other, running a thumb along the seam of the housing the way you'd feel for a port you were sure had to be there. The port he's hunting for exists only in his head. The board takes power and a six-pin harness that only their own test rig will drive, and that's the whole of it. He tries the next unit down the bench, and the one after that, three boards with the same blank seam running down each one, and on the fourth he puts the stick back in his pocket, picks the rag back up off his shoulder, and finishes the row at the same pace as if that were the whole job. He wipes the same bench twice on his way past it, the way anyone padding out an evening's hours might, his eyes on the cart the whole way past the office where the light's still on.

Nadia handles it herself, the way she handles most things on that floor. She crosses the room in the boots she wore to fix the drill press that morning and stands at the end of the bench until he looks up and sees her there.

"Whatever's in your pocket," she says. "On the bench."

He sets it down. A plain stick, the casing blank.

"You're done here. Leave your badge at the desk on your way out."

He hands his badge across in silence, and she lets the silence stand, the question of how she knew left exactly where it is. He pushes the cart toward the door at the same pace he'd have used to skip the break room on any other night, and she watches him the whole way before she picks the stick up off the bench with two fingers and drops it in a drawer.
